| Mishima -> Kyoto Shinkansen | [14](#day-14---tuesday-october-20-2026---kawaguchiko--kyoto) | Mishima Station, Shizuoka, to Kyoto Station, Kyoto (Tokaido Shinkansen)<br>Link: [global.jr-central.co.jp](https://global.jr-central.co.jp/en/) | The trip's one full intercity transfer leg: an unreserved Hikari covering Mishima to Kyoto in 1h50, at cruising speeds around 285 km/h. ¥10,780/adult; infants ride free and the stroller is exempt from the oversized-baggage reservation rule. The ride itself is smooth, seated downtime after a morning of buses and transfers, a contrast to the walking-heavy days on either side of it. |
| Sagano Romantic Train (Torokko) | [17](#day-17---friday-october-23-2026---kyoto) | Torokko Saga Station, adjacent to JR Saga-Arashiyama Station, Ukyo-ku, Kyoto (exact street address unverified)<br>Link: [sagano-kanko.co.jp](https://www.sagano-kanko.co.jp/en/) | A slow, open-window sightseeing train on a former freight line, running one-way through the Hozugawa gorge with forested cliffs and river rapids close alongside the tracks - a genuinely different view of Arashiyama than any of its temples give. ¥880/adult; reserve ahead, since departures run roughly hourly and seats sell out on weekends. Boarding is a small step up from a low platform, manageable with the stroller folded rather than wheeled on. |
| Fushimi Jikkokubune canal boat | [22](#day-22---wednesday-october-28-2026---kyoto-day-trip-to-the-fushimi-sake-district) | Boarding dock by Bekkan Terrace, Chushojima, Fushimi-ku, Kyoto (address unverified)<br>Link: [kyoto-fushimi.or.jp](https://kyoto-fushimi.or.jp/fune/) | A round trip in a jikkokubune, the flat-bottomed wooden cargo boat that once carried rice and sake down these canals to Osaka, passing the backs of the brewery warehouses and through the Miyuki-bashi lock. It explains why a sake industry grew here at all - Fushimi was a river port before it was a brewing town, and the canal was the road. ¥1,900/adult, about 55 minutes, seated throughout. Reservations are online-only, no phone or email, closing at midnight two days before the date; it does not run Mondays. |
| Kyoto -> Hiroshima Shinkansen | [24](#day-24---friday-october-30-2026---kyoto-to-hiroshima) | Kyoto Station, Kyoto, to Hiroshima Station, Hiroshima (Sanyo Shinkansen)<br>Link: [westjr.co.jp](https://www.westjr.co.jp/global/en/train/shinkansen/tokaido-sanyo-shinkansen/) | An unreserved Hikari/Sakura run of 1h40 on the Sanyo Shinkansen, the segment's first of two big intercity legs. Walk-up fare is ¥10,770/adult, but this leg is covered by the JR Kansai-Hiroshima Area Pass bought the same morning, which pays for itself almost entirely on this one ride. Infants ride free and the stroller travels without a reservation. |
| JR Miyajima ferry (round trip) | [25](#day-25---saturday-october-31-2026---miyajima-itsukushima) | Miyajimaguchi Pier, Hatsukaichi, to Miyajima Pier, Hatsukaichi, Hiroshima<br>Link: [jr-miyajimaferry.co.jp](https://jr-miyajimaferry.co.jp/en/) | A 10-minute crossing that passes closest to Itsukushima Shrine's floating torii gate of any of the island's ferries, so the approach by water is itself part of the sight rather than just a way to reach it. ¥400 round trip walk-up, but covered by the JR Kansai-Hiroshima Area Pass. Infants ride free, same as on JR trains. |
| Miyajima Ropeway | [25](#day-25---saturday-october-31-2026---miyajima-itsukushima) | Momijidani Station, Miyajima-cho, Hatsukaichi (address unverified)<br>Link: [miyajima-ropeway.info](https://miyajima-ropeway.info/english/) | A two-stage cable car climbing most of the way up Mt. Misen, Miyajima's sacred mountain, ending at a viewpoint over the Inland Sea and its islands - a scenic gondola ride substituting for the stone-stepped summit trail, which is carrier-only and unsuited to two adults managing two under-twos on steep stairs. ¥2,000/adult round trip. Only 2 of 3 adults go up, one carrying the toddler, while the third rests with the stroller and the other infant at the base station. |
| Hiroden streetcar | [24](#day-24---friday-october-30-2026---kyoto-to-hiroshima), [26](#day-26---sunday-november-1-2026---hiroshima-city-day) | Hiroshima (citywide network; boarding points include Hiroshima Station and Genbaku Dome-mae)<br>Link: [hiroden.co.jp](https://www.hiroden.co.jp/en/) | One of the last big tram networks left running in Japan, with more than 35km of track and a fleet that deliberately keeps older cars in service rather than retiring them - some surviving pre-war units still run alongside newer low-floor trams on the same lines. A flat citywide fare of ¥220 per ride makes it the easy, cheap way to move between Peace Memorial Park, Shukkei-en and Hiroshima Castle; infants ride free. |
| Hiroshima -> Shin-Osaka Shinkansen | [27](#day-27---monday-november-2-2026---hiroshima-to-osaka) | Hiroshima Station, Hiroshima, to Shin-Osaka Station, Osaka (Sanyo Shinkansen)<br>Link: [westjr.co.jp](https://www.westjr.co.jp/global/en/train/shinkansen/tokaido-sanyo-shinkansen/) | The segment's second big intercity leg, an unreserved Hikari/Sakura covering Hiroshima to Shin-Osaka in 1h30. Walk-up fare is ¥9,710/adult, again covered by the JR Kansai-Hiroshima Area Pass bought on Day 24, using up the pass's last day of validity. Infants ride free. |
| Tombori River Cruise | [29](#day-29---wednesday-november-4-2026---osaka) | Tazaemonbashi Pier, by the Don Quijote store, Dotonbori, Chuo-ku, Osaka (pier location; no street address)<br>Link: [ipponmatsu.co.jp](https://www.ipponmatsu.co.jp/cruise/tombori.html) | A 20-minute narrated boat ride along the Dotonbori canal with a live guide, passing underneath the same illuminated signs - including the Glico running man - already seen on foot, but from the water and after dark, when the signs are lit. ¥2,000/adult, infants free. It runs on scheduled hourly and half-hourly departures rather than a walk-up guarantee, and boarding is via a short ramp or steps at the pier. |

### Views and observation decks

| What | Day | Where | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tokyo Skytree Tembo Deck | [2](#day-2---thursday-october-8-2026---tokyo) | 1-1-2 Oshiage, Sumida-ku, Tokyo 131-0045<br>Link: [en.tokyo-skytree.jp](https://en.tokyo-skytree.jp/) | The first observation deck (350m) of the tallest structure in Japan (634m total), with glass-floor sections and a 360-degree view over the Kanto plain - Mt. Fuji is visible on a clear day. ¥2,500/adult; advance timed tickets are recommended to skip the counter queue. Elevators throughout, fully stroller and infant-friendly. |
| Tokyo Tower Main Deck | [4](#day-4---saturday-october-10-2026---tokyo) | 4-2-8 Shibakoen, Minato-ku, Tokyo (address per site research; postal code unverified)<br>Link: [en.tokyotower.co.jp](https://en.tokyotower.co.jp/) | The Main Deck (150m) of Tokyo's original 1958 broadcast tower - lower and more central than Skytree, giving a different classic view of the city rather than a redundant one. ¥1,500/adult, elevator-served, no stairs. Worth doing on its own terms even a few days after visiting Skytree. |
| Landmark Tower Sky Garden | [9](#day-9---thursday-october-15-2026---yokohama-day-trip) | 2-2-1 Minatomirai, Nishi-ku, Yokohama, Kanagawa 220-0012<br>Link: [yokohama-landmark.jp](https://www.yokohama-landmark.jp/skygarden/) | The 69th-floor observation deck of what was, for decades, Japan's tallest building, reached by a high-speed elevator in about 40 seconds, with sweeping views over Minato Mirai's harbor skyline and Yokohama Bay (Mt. Fuji visible on a clear day). ¥1,200/adult. Fully indoor and stroller/infant-friendly. |
| Chureito Pagoda viewpoint | [11](#day-11---saturday-october-17-2026---tokyo--kawaguchiko) | 3353-1 Arakura, Fujiyoshida, Yamanashi 403-0011<br>Link: [en.fujiyoshida.net](https://en.fujiyoshida.net/) | A five-story vermilion pagoda on a hillside above Lake Kawaguchi's basin with Mt. Fuji rising directly behind it - the single most photographed view in the Fuji Five Lakes region. Free, reached via 398 stone steps or a longer, gentler zig-zag slope path, both carrier-only with no ramp or elevator anywhere on the route. |
| Kawaguchiko Tenjozan Ropeway summit | [12](#day-12---sunday-october-18-2026---kawaguchiko) | 1163 Azagawa, Fujikawaguchiko-machi, Minamitsuru-gun, Yamanashi 401-0303<br>Link: [mtfujiropeway.jp](https://www.mtfujiropeway.jp/en/) | The summit deck reached by the ropeway above, with an unbroken panorama of Mt. Fuji across the full width of Lake Kawaguchi - on a clear day the best elevated vantage point on the Kawaguchiko leg. ¥1,000/adult round trip, infants free. Ride late morning to keep the wait short before midday crowds build. |
| Miyajima Ropeway summit | [25](#day-25---saturday-october-31-2026---miyajima-itsukushima) | Shishiiwa Station, Mt. Misen, Miyajima, Hatsukaichi (address unverified)<br>Link: [miyajima-ropeway.info](https://miyajima-ropeway.info/english/) | A viewpoint over the Inland Sea and its islands reached by the two-stage cable car, standing in for the stone-stepped walk to Mt. Misen's actual 535m summit, which this trip skips. ¥2,000/adult round trip. Only 2 of 3 adults go up, one carrying the toddler, while the third rests with the stroller at the base station. |
| Hiroshima Orizuru Tower | [26](#day-26---sunday-november-1-2026---hiroshima-city-day) | 1-2-1 Otemachi, Naka-ku, Hiroshima 730-0051<br>Link: [orizurutower.jp](https://www.orizurutower.jp/en/) | A modern tower directly across from the Atomic Bomb Dome, with an open-air rooftop deck looking back over Peace Park and the city, plus a spiral ramp down through a glass wall where visitors fold and drop a paper crane into the atrium below. ¥2,200/adult, a deliberately lighter, hands-on stop right after the Peace Memorial Museum. Elevator access throughout, fully stroller-accessible. |
| Tsutenkaku Tower | [29](#day-29---wednesday-november-4-2026---osaka) | 1-18-6 Ebisuhigashi, Naniwa-ku, Osaka 556-0002<br>Link: [tsutenkaku.co.jp](https://www.tsutenkaku.co.jp/) | A 103m steel observation tower first built in 1912 and rebuilt in 1956, the centerpiece of the retro Shinsekai entertainment district - a smaller, more old-fashioned stand-in for Osaka's newer towers, and this trip's own "city from above" stop. ¥900/adult, elevator-served; infants free. |
| Shibuya Scramble Crossing and Hachiko statue | [5](#day-5---sunday-october-11-2026---tokyo) | Outside Shibuya Stn Hachiko Exit, Shibuya-ku (address unverified)<br>Link: [gotokyo.org](https://www.gotokyo.org/en/spot/78/index.html) | The famously chaotic five-way pedestrian crossing where up to 3,000 people cross at once when the light changes, next to the bronze statue of Hachiko, the dog who waited at the station for his deceased owner for years. Free, a five-minute "see it to believe it" stop rather than a place to linger - best watched from the station walkway or a window rather than from inside the crowd with a stroller. |
| Kawaguchiko Tenjozan Panoramic Ropeway | [12](#day-12---sunday-october-18-2026---kawaguchiko) | 1163 Azagawa, Fujikawaguchiko-machi, Minamitsuru-gun, Yamanashi 401-0303<br>Link: [mtfujiropeway.jp](https://www.mtfujiropeway.jp/en/) | The same cable car and summit view as above, climbing about 400m up Mt. Tenjo in under three minutes to a deck overlooking the full width of Lake Kawaguchi and Mt. Fuji beyond it. ¥1,000/adult round trip, infants free. Morning is the reliable window for a clear view of the mountain before afternoon cloud builds. |

### Things aimed at the toddlers

| What | Day | Where | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|
| Sumida Aquarium | [2](#day-2---thursday-october-8-2026---tokyo) | Tokyo Solamachi, 1-1-2 Oshiage, Sumida-ku, Tokyo 131-0045<br>Link: [en.sumida-aquarium.com](https://en.sumida-aquarium.com/) | An indoor aquarium inside the Skytree Town mall, built around an open-topped penguin and fur seal pool you can walk right up to, plus jellyfish tanks and a touch pool. Compact and low-stimulation rather than spectacular - a good indoor stop for toddlers after Senso-ji's crowds. ¥2,900/adult, fully stroller-accessible with elevators and on-site changing facilities. |
| Ueno Zoo | [3](#day-3---friday-october-9-2026---tokyo) | 9-83 Uenokoen, Taito-ku, Tokyo 110-8711<br>Link: [tokyo-zoo.net](https://www.tokyo-zoo.net/en/ueno/) | Japan's oldest zoo, opened 1882, on paved, gently sloped paths inside Ueno Park. **There are no pandas**: the last two, twin cubs Xiao Xiao and Lei Lei, were flown back to China in late January 2026, leaving Japan without a single giant panda for the first time since 1972 - don't build up "the pandas" beforehand. What's actually there instead: Asian elephants, western lowland gorillas, Sumatran tigers, polar bears, and capybara, plus a five-storey pagoda inside the zoo grounds. ¥600/adult, stroller-friendly throughout, infants free. |
| Yoyogi Park | [5](#day-5---sunday-october-11-2026---tokyo) | Yoyogikamizonocho, Shibuya-ku, adjacent to Meiji Jingu (nearest station Harajuku; address unverified)<br>Link: [tokyo-park.or.jp](https://www.tokyo-park.or.jp/park/yoyogi/) | Tokyo's largest and most famous city park, wide open lawns beside Meiji Jingu's forest. Free, unstructured downtime - good for toddlers to run on grass after a morning of gravel paths and shrine etiquette. Flat paved and grass paths throughout, fully stroller-friendly. |
| teamLab Planets | [8](#day-8---wednesday-october-14-2026---tokyo-odaiba--toyosu) | Toyosu 6-1-16, Koto-ku, Tokyo 135-0061<br>Link: [teamlab.art](https://www.teamlab.art/e/planets/) | A barefoot, walk-through digital art museum: visitors wade ankle-deep through a mirrored water room and move through immersive projection-mapped spaces. Genuinely unlike anything else on the trip. ¥5,300/adult; bring a full change of clothes since guests get wet to the knee, and strollers are left at entrance lockers, the space navigated on foot or in the carrier. |
| Inokashira Park + small zoo corner | [10](#day-10---friday-october-16-2026---tokyo) | Inokashira Park, Musashino-shi/Mitaka-shi (nearest station Kichijoji or Inokashira-koen; address unverified)<br>Link: [tokyo-zoo.net](https://www.tokyo-zoo.net/en/inokashira/) | A large pond-centered park adjoining the Ghibli Museum, known for swan-shaped paddle boats and a small, free-to-enter zoo corner with a paid small-animal and elephant enclosure (¥500/adult). An easy fallback if the Ghibli Museum lottery doesn't come through. Paved paths around the pond, stroller-friendly. |
| Nara Park deer | [20](#day-20---monday-october-26-2026---kyoto-day-trip-to-nara) | Nara Park (district; address unverified)<br>Link: [narashikanko.or.jp](https://narashikanko.or.jp/en/feature/deer) | Roughly 1,200 wild sika deer that have wandered Nara Park freely for over a thousand years, historically protected as messengers of the local Shinto gods. They are not fenced and will bow their heads for a cracker, a learned begging cue - but they push, butt, and nibble at clothing once they smell food, worth knowing with a toddler at their eye height. Free to watch; ¥200 per pack of crackers to feed them by hand. |
| Miyajima Public Aquarium | [25](#day-25---saturday-october-31-2026---miyajima-itsukushima) | 10-3 Miyajima-cho, Hatsukaichi, Hiroshima 739-0588<br>Link: [miyajima-aqua.jp](https://www.miyajima-aqua.jp/) | A mid-size public aquarium a ten-minute walk from Itsukushima Shrine, built around Seto Inland Sea marine life plus a penguin and sea lion show and a hands-on touch pool. A good pressure-release stop after two temple/shrine visits rather than a world-class aquarium. ¥1,420/adult, indoor, climate-controlled, and flat-floored - the easiest stroller stretch of the day. |
| Miyajima deer | [25](#day-25---saturday-october-31-2026---miyajima-itsukushima) | Miyajima, Hatsukaichi (district; address unverified)<br>Link: [miyajima.or.jp](https://www.miyajima.or.jp/english/nature/nature_animal.html) | About five hundred wild sika deer that roam Miyajima island freely, considered sacred in Shinto belief. Like Nara's, they are not fenced and are famous for "bowing," a learned cue for the shika senbei crackers sold around the island for about ¥200 a pack - but they push and nose at food and bags, so keep crackers out of a toddler's reach and hands empty once they're gone. |
| Anpanman Children's Museum & Mall | [28](#day-28---tuesday-november-3-2026---osaka) | 1-6-2 Higashikawasakicho, Chuo-ku, Kobe 650-0044<br>Link: [kobe-anpanman.jp](https://www.kobe-anpanman.jp/en/) | A two-zone complex built around Anpanman, the toddler-recognized superhero: a free ground-floor mall with a themed bakery, food court, and souvenir shops, and a paid 2nd-floor museum of play structures, a stage show, and soft-play elements sized for small bodies. **Unlike almost every other site on this trip, admission is charged from age 1** - roughly ¥2,000-2,500 per person - so both toddlers here are paying visitors, not free infants. A web ticket for a specific date and time is required. Indoor, stroller-parked-at-door, fully step-free. |
| Deer senbei at Nara Park | [20](#day-20---monday-october-26-2026---kyoto-day-trip-to-nara) | Nara Park (district; address unverified)<br>Link: [narashikanko.or.jp](https://narashikanko.or.jp/en/feature/deer) | Packs of unglazed rice crackers sold by park vendors let you hand-feed Nara's wild deer, many of whom genuinely bow their heads for one. The deer get pushy once they know you're holding food, sometimes butting or nibbling at clothing, so keep the pack out of a toddler's reach and show empty hands once it's gone. ¥200 per pack, ¥600 for three. |

### Shopping

| What | Day | Where | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|
| Nakamise-dori | [2](#day-2---thursday-october-8-2026---tokyo) | 2-3-1 Asakusa, Taito-ku, Tokyo 111-0032<br>Link: [senso-ji.jp](https://www.senso-ji.jp/) | Senso-ji's covered approach street, about 250m of souvenir and snack stalls running from the Kaminarimon gate to the temple itself. Free to browse. Genuinely too dense for a stroller by mid-morning, so plan on the carrier. |
| Ameyoko | [3](#day-3---friday-october-9-2026---tokyo) | Ameyoko market, Taito-ku, Tokyo (address unverified)<br>Link: [ameyoko.net](https://www.ameyoko.net/) | A street market between Ueno and Okachimachi stations mixing cut-price clothing and cosmetics with seafood stalls and street food, a legacy of its postwar black-market origins. Free to browse. |
| Akihabara | [3](#day-3---friday-october-9-2026---tokyo) | Sotokanda, Chiyoda-ku, Tokyo - Mandarake Complex, 3-11-12 Sotokanda; Super Potato, Kitabayashi Bldg 3F-5F, 1-11-2 Sotokanda<br>Link: [mandarake.co.jp](https://www.mandarake.co.jp/) [superpotato.com](https://www.superpotato.com/) | Tokyo's "electric town": Radio Kaikan and Gachapon Kaikan for capsule toys, Mandarake Complex's eight floors of manga, figures and Gunpla, Super Potato's retro-game floors, and Yodobashi Camera. Free to browse. Ground floors are stroller-workable in short bursts; Mandarake's upper floors and Super Potato's 3F-5F run on narrow stairs and a small lift, so one adult peels off there while the stroller waits below. |
| Kappabashi Kitchenware Street | [6](#day-6---monday-october-12-2026---tokyo-sports-day) | Kappabashi Kitchen Street, Nishi-Asakusa, Taito-ku (specific studio address unverified)<br>Link: [kappabashi.or.jp](https://www.kappabashi.or.jp/en/) | Tokyo's wholesale restaurant-supply street, about 15 minutes on foot from Ueno - the place where the uncannily realistic plastic food displayed in restaurant windows across Japan is actually made and sold, alongside knives, crockery and every other piece of a restaurant kitchen. Free to browse. A hands-on plastic food-sample workshop here (see Crafts) needs adult hands for the hot wax. |
| Takeshita Street + Laforet Harajuku | [5](#day-5---sunday-october-11-2026---tokyo) | Takeshita-dori, Jingumae, Shibuya-ku, Tokyo 150-0001; Laforet Harajuku, 1-11-6 Jingumae, Shibuya-ku, Tokyo 150-0001<br>Link: [laforet.ne.jp](https://www.laforet.ne.jp/en/) | A 350m pedestrian street of roughly 130 shops - teen fast fashion, crepe stands, purikura booths - the most recognizable image of Harajuku. It is narrow and genuinely packed by early afternoon, worse on weekends, so this is carrier rather than stroller terrain. The old street-level Lolita and gothic Lolita cosplay gathering on the nearby Jingu-bashi bridge faded years ago; what survives of that fashion is retail, in boutiques inside Laforet - Angelic Pretty, Baby the Stars Shine Bright, and the gothic-leaning Alice and the Pirates. Free to walk through; Laforet is open 11:00-20:00 with no fixed holidays. |
| Shibuya PARCO | [5](#day-5---sunday-october-11-2026---tokyo) | Shibuya PARCO, 6F, 15-1 Udagawacho, Shibuya-ku, Tokyo 150-8377<br>Link: [en.shibuya.parco.jp](https://en.shibuya.parco.jp/) | One mall floor sharing three fandoms: Pokemon Center Shibuya, the Jump Shop (Shonen Jump merchandise), and Nintendo Tokyo (official Nintendo goods, no console games sold). Free to enter all three. Elevator-served and fully stroller-friendly, a good weatherproof stand-in for the day's other Shibuya stops. |
| Nishiki Market + Daimaru depachika | [15](#day-15---wednesday-october-21-2026---kyoto) | Nishikikoji-dori, Nakagyo-ku, Kyoto 604-8054 (Daimaru Kyoto depachika basement, Nakagyo-ku)<br>Link: [kyoto-nishiki.or.jp](https://www.kyoto-nishiki.or.jp/en/) | Kyoto's 400-year-old covered food arcade - seafood, pickles, knives, tea and skewered snacks from about 130 shops - paired here with the basement food hall (depachika) of the neighboring Daimaru department store for a bento top-up. Free to browse. Several Nishiki stalls now ask visitors not to eat while walking; the covered floor is stroller-workable but narrow and crowded at midday. |
| Omamori charms + goshuincho stamp | [15](#day-15---wednesday-october-21-2026---kyoto) | Fushimi Inari Taisha stalls, Fushimi-ku, near JR Inari Station (address unverified)<br>Link: [inari.jp](https://inari.jp/en/) | Omamori are small charms sold at shrine and temple stalls, each blessed for one specific wish - safe travel, health, good luck. A goshuincho is a blank accordion-fold book carried between sites to collect goshuin, a priest's hand-brushed calligraphy stamp marking the visit, traditionally a pilgrim's record rather than a souvenir. A five-minute stall stop, about ¥500 per charm, not a destination in itself. |
| Sannenzaka/Ninenzaka | [18](#day-18---saturday-october-24-2026---kyoto) | Sannenzaka and Ninenzaka slopes, Higashiyama-ku, near the Kiyomizu-michi bus stop (address unverified) | Two connected stone-paved lanes climbing toward Kiyomizu-dera, lined with preserved wooden shopfronts, teahouses and souvenir stores - the closest thing to a preserved Edo-period streetscape in central Kyoto. Free to walk, best early or late for fewer crowds. The paving is old, uneven flagstone on a slope: hard going for a stroller, easier carried or walked slowly by hand. |
| Kuromon Ichiba Market | [29](#day-29---wednesday-november-4-2026---osaka) | Kuromon Ichiba Market, 2-4-1 Nipponbashi, Chuo-ku, Osaka 542-0073<br>Link: [kuromon.com](https://kuromon.com/en/) | A covered market of about 170 stalls, once a wholesale fish and produce market and now dominated by grill-it-and-eat-it single portions - nigiri, wagyu skewers, tamagoyaki, oysters - priced for visitors rather than a neighborhood greengrocer. Free to enter and browse. |
| Den Den Town | [29](#day-29---wednesday-november-4-2026---osaka) | Nipponbashi/Ota Road, Naniwa-ku/Chuo-ku, Osaka (district, no single address) | Osaka's own anime and hobby-shop district, a roughly 1km strip along Sakaisuji and the parallel Ota Road with over 150 shops - Animate, Yellow Submarine, Super Potato, K-Books and other figure, Gunpla, card and doujinshi stores. It shares some chains with Akihabara but reads smaller, less crowded and more collector-hobbyist. Free to browse; flat, wide shopping street, stroller-workable outside peak hours. |
| Dotonbori/Shinsaibashi | [27](#day-27---monday-november-2-2026---hiroshima-to-osaka), [29](#day-29---wednesday-november-4-2026---osaka) | Dotonbori canal, Chuo-ku, Osaka (canal-side pedestrian strip, no single street address); Shinsaibashi-suji, Chuo-ku, Osaka<br>Link: [dotonbori.or.jp](http://www.dotonbori.or.jp/en/) [global.shinsaibashi.or.jp](https://global.shinsaibashi.or.jp/en/) | Osaka's best-known nightlife and shopping strip: Dotonbori's canal-side promenade of oversized illuminated signs, most famously the Glico running man, feeding straight into the covered Shinsaibashi-suji arcade of fashion and department stores. Free to walk. Level and paved throughout, stroller-workable on the main routes, but expect dense, slow-moving evening crowds on both. |

---

## Appendix B: Food index

Every dish the trip eats, by type, with the day it happens on and where.
Regional specialities are listed separately at the end.

#### Noodles

Japan treats "noodles" as three separate foods, not three versions of one dish. Udon are
thick, soft, chewy wheat noodles. Soba are thin, brownish-grey buckwheat noodles with a
nutty flavor, often served cold with a small cup of dipping sauce rather than in soup.
Ramen are thin wheat noodles served in a rich meat or fish broth, a much younger dish
than udon or soba and closer in spirit to a full meal than a noodle side.

| What | Day | Where | Typical price | kcal | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kake udon with chicken tempura | [1](#day-1---wednesday-october-7-2026---tokyo-narita-arrival) | Fast-casual udon counter, Ueno Stn concourse, Taito-ku (exact shop not recorded) | ~¥400-600/adult (approx; bought as part of a ¥1,200 combo for three adults that also included an onigiri rice ball) | ~450-500 kcal (approx; the full combo runs 650 kcal/adult) | Kake udon is the plainest form of udon and usually the cheapest item on the menu: thick wheat noodles sitting in a bowl of hot, mild soy-and-dashi broth, with nothing else added until you drop in the tempura on top - here a piece of battered, deep-fried chicken. Eaten hot, with chopsticks for the noodles and a spoon or the bowl itself for the broth; slurping is normal and not rude. No raw fish, and soft enough for a toddler once cooled, though the tempura batter and broth carry gluten and soy. |
| Shoyu ramen with gyoza | [2](#day-2---thursday-october-8-2026---tokyo) | Ramen counter, Tokyo Solamachi food court, Sumida-ku<br>Link: [en.www.tokyo-solamachi.jp](https://en.www.tokyo-solamachi.jp/) (mall site; the individual ramen counter isn't named) | ~¥1,050/adult (¥3,200 for three adults) | ~750 kcal | Shoyu (soy sauce) ramen is wheat noodles in a clear brown broth seasoned with soy sauce, usually built on a chicken or pork stock, topped with sliced pork, a soft-boiled egg, spring onion and nori seaweed. It comes with a small side of gyoza, pan-fried pork-and-vegetable dumplings with a crisp bottom and soft steamed top, dipped in a soy-vinegar sauce. Both are eaten hot and immediately; ramen broth cools fast and is meant to be finished quickly. Contains wheat, soy, egg and pork - not suitable for a pork-free diet. |
| Houtou | [12](#day-12---sunday-october-18-2026---kawaguchiko) | Hoto Fudou or a similar local houtou specialist, Fujikawaguchiko area, Yamanashi (exact branch unconfirmed)<br>Link: [houtou-fudou.jp](http://www.houtou-fudou.jp/) | ~¥1,400/adult | ~750 kcal | Houtou is Yamanashi prefecture's own noodle dish, not found as standard fare elsewhere in Japan: flat, wide, chewy wheat noodles simmered directly in a miso broth with chunks of kabocha (Japanese pumpkin) and other root vegetables until the broth thickens slightly from the starch. It's brought to the table still bubbling in the cast-iron pot it was cooked in, so it stays hot for a while and needs a minute before the first spoonful. Vegetarian at most houtou shops (the broth is miso and vegetable, not fish or meat stock, though this varies by kitchen), and mild enough for a toddler once cooled - the flat noodles are easy to bite through. |
| Yudofu teishoku | [17](#day-17---friday-october-23-2026---kyoto) | Restaurant near Tenryu-ji, Saga-Arashiyama, Ukyo-ku (exact restaurant unverified) | ~¥1,000/adult (¥3,100 for three adults) | ~700 kcal | Not a noodle dish itself, but this is the meal actually eaten that day: a teishoku (set meal) built around yudofu, a Kyoto/Arashiyama specialty of plain tofu cubes gently simmered tableside in a pot of kombu (kelp) broth, eaten dipped in a soy-based sauce with grated ginger or spring onion, alongside rice and pickles. Served hot, in a small pot you serve yourself from; very mild and easy for a toddler once cooled, since the tofu itself is unseasoned. Contains soy; naturally vegetarian. |
| Zaru soba | [18](#day-18---saturday-october-24-2026---kyoto) | Shop in the Sannenzaka/Ninenzaka lanes, Higashiyama-ku, Kyoto (exact shop unverified) | ~¥650-750/adult (approx; bought alongside a matcha soft-serve cone for ¥2,300 total for three adults) | ~500-550 kcal (approx; the noodles plus soft-serve run 650 kcal/adult) | Zaru soba is served cold, which surprises people expecting a hot noodle soup: chilled buckwheat noodles are piled on a bamboo tray (the zaru) to drain, with a small cup of cold, salty-sweet dipping sauce (tsuyu) served alongside rather than poured over. You pick up a small bundle of noodles with chopsticks, dip the ends in the sauce, and eat - not the whole bowl at once. Buckwheat gives soba a nutty flavor unlike wheat udon or ramen; most Japanese soba flour is blended with some wheat flour, so it is not reliably gluten-free. |
| Tonkotsu ramen | [10](#day-10---friday-october-16-2026---tokyo) | Ichiran, Shinjuku (branch address unverified)<br>Link: [en.ichiran.com](https://en.ichiran.com/) | ~¥980/adult (¥2,940 for three adults) | 750 kcal | Tonkotsu means a pork-bone broth, simmered for hours until it turns opaque and rich rather than clear. Ichiran serves one dish only, and the way you order is the point: you buy a ticket from a vending machine at the door, then fill in a paper form choosing broth richness, garlic, spring onion, chilli and noodle firmness. You eat in an individual partitioned booth with a bamboo screen that lifts only for the bowl to arrive, which is either the appeal or the oddity depending on the diner. Contains pork; the booths seat one, so a lap infant works but a stroller does not fit at the counter. |
| Matcha soba | [21](#day-21---tuesday-october-27-2026---kyoto-day-trip-to-uji) | Tea house near Uji Bridge, Uji-shi (name unrecorded) | ~¥1,200/adult (approx; part of a ¥3,600 tea-house set meal for three adults that also included tea-leaf tempura and ochazuke) | ~700 kcal (approx, for the full set) | Uji is Japan's best-known green tea region, and matcha soba is the local twist on buckwheat noodles: matcha (powdered green tea) is kneaded into the dough, giving the noodles a vivid green color and a light, slightly bitter tea flavor. This set also included tea-leaf tempura (whole tea leaves battered and deep-fried) and ochazuke (green tea poured over a bowl of rice, eaten almost like a light soup). All served hot in this case; contains wheat and gluten from both the noodles and the tempura batter. |
| Zaru soba | [23](#day-23---thursday-october-29-2026---kyoto-low-key-day--packing) | Nishijin neighbourhood, Kamigyo-ku, Kyoto (shop unverified) | ~¥700-800/adult (approx; bought with a side of vegetable tempura for ¥2,400 total for three adults) | ~550 kcal (approx; the set with tempura runs 650 kcal/adult) | The same cold buckwheat-noodles-and-dipping-sauce dish as Day 18's zaru soba (see above), here paired with a side of vegetable tempura instead of soft-serve. Eaten cold, dipping small bundles of noodles into the sauce cup rather than pouring it over the tray. Contains wheat (soba flour blend and the tempura batter) and, if oil is shared with other fried items, possibly shellfish traces - worth asking about at a stall serving mixed tempura. |
| Tonkotsu ramen with gyoza | [22](#day-22---wednesday-october-28-2026---kyoto-day-trip-to-the-fushimi-sake-district) | Ippudo, near Shijo-Omiya, Kyoto<br>Link: [ippudo.com](https://www.ippudo.com/) | ~¥900/adult (¥2,700 for three adults) | ~800 kcal (approx, for ramen and gyoza together) | Tonkotsu means pork-bone broth: pork bones are boiled for hours until they break down into a cloudy, opaque, rich, almost creamy stock, the defining style of Kyushu-origin chains like Ippudo. The noodles here are thin and firm, topped with sliced pork belly, wood-ear mushroom and spring onion, with a side of pan-fried gyoza dumplings. Many ramen chains, Ippudo included, take orders from a ticket vending machine just inside the door - you pick and pay for your bowl before sitting down and hand the ticket to staff. Eaten hot; contains pork, wheat and soy, and is one of the richer, more filling dishes on this list. |
| Tempura udon set | [24](#day-24---friday-october-30-2026---kyoto-to-hiroshima) | Teishoku counter, Hiroshima Stn concourse, Minami-ku (exact shop unverified) | ~¥650/adult (¥1,950 for three adults) | ~650 kcal | A counter-service teishoku (set meal): a bowl of udon (thick wheat noodles) in hot broth topped with tempura (battered, deep-fried) vegetables or shrimp, served together with a small bowl of rice and pickles. Everything is eaten hot; it's a fast, filling station-concourse lunch rather than a sit-down restaurant meal. Contains wheat and gluten in both the noodles and the tempura batter, plus shellfish if the tempura includes shrimp. |
| Tonkotsu ramen with gyoza | [24](#day-24---friday-october-30-2026---kyoto-to-hiroshima) | Counter-seating ramen shop, Hondori arcade, Naka-ku, Hiroshima (exact shop unverified) | ~¥850/adult (¥2,550 for three adults) | ~750 kcal (approx, for ramen and gyoza together) | The same rich, opaque pork-bone tonkotsu broth described under Day 22's Ippudo entry above, at an independent counter shop in Hiroshima's Hondori arcade rather than a national chain, again served with a side of pan-fried gyoza. Eaten hot; contains pork, wheat and soy. |
| Kake udon | [26](#day-26---sunday-november-1-2026---hiroshima-city-day) | Teishoku counter, Hondori arcade, Naka-ku, Hiroshima (exact shop unverified) | ~¥500-600/adult (approx; bought with a chicken tempura topping and a rice ball for ¥2,250 total for three adults) | ~450 kcal (approx; the full lunch runs 700 kcal/adult) | Kake udon again - see Day 1 above: thick wheat noodles in a plain, mild hot broth, the simplest and usually cheapest udon order on any menu. This time it came with a chicken tempura topping and a separate rice ball rather than an onigiri from the same counter. Hot, mild, and easy for a toddler to eat once cooled; contains wheat and soy. |
| Kake udon | [27](#day-27---monday-november-2-2026---hiroshima-to-osaka) | Self-serve udon counter near Namba Stn, Osaka (for example, a Marugame Seimen branch; exact shop unconfirmed)<br>Link: [jp.marugame.com](https://jp.marugame.com/) | ~¥500-600/adult (approx; bought with a chicken tempura topping and an onigiri for ¥2,250 total for three adults) | ~450 kcal (approx; the full lunch runs 700 kcal/adult) | The same plain kake udon as above, at a self-serve chain-style udon shop: at places like Marugame Seimen you collect a bowl of noodles from a counter, add your own tempura and other toppings from a display case as you move down the line, and pay at a register at the end, cafeteria-style, rather than ordering from a vending machine or a server. Hot and mild; contains wheat and soy. |

#### Rice dishes

| What | Day | Where | Typical price | kcal | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Katsudon | [4](#day-4---saturday-october-10-2026---tokyo) | Teishoku counter, Shibakoen/Hamamatsucho area, Minato-ku (exact shop unverified) | ~¥870/adult (¥2,600 for three adults) | ~750 kcal | Katsudon is a breaded, deep-fried pork cutlet (tonkatsu) simmered briefly with sliced onion in a sweet soy-and-dashi broth, then bound together with beaten egg poured over the top just until it sets, and served over a bowl of rice - the crumb coating on the cutlet stays crisp on top and softens where it meets the egg and broth. Eaten hot with chopsticks, working down through the egg and pork into the rice beneath. Contains pork, egg, wheat (in the panko coating) and soy; hearty enough that a toddler can share the rice, egg and small deboned pieces of pork. |
| Katsu curry | [20](#day-20---monday-october-26-2026---kyoto-day-trip-to-nara) | CoCo Ichibanya, Shijo-Omiya neighbourhood, Kyoto<br>Link: [ichibanya.co.jp](https://www.ichibanya.co.jp/english/) | ~¥900/adult (¥2,700 for three adults) | ~800 kcal | Japanese curry is its own thing, milder and sweeter than Indian or Thai curry, made from a roux-based sauce (closer in texture and flavor to a thick brown gravy than a spiced curry) poured over rice, here topped with the same breaded, deep-fried pork cutlet used in katsudon. CoCo Ichibanya lets diners choose their own spice level from mild to very hot and add extra toppings; this order was kept mild. Eaten hot with a spoon; contains wheat (in both the roux and the cutlet's breading) and pork, and the mild setting is fine for a toddler to taste. |
| Gyudon | [17](#day-17---friday-october-23-2026---kyoto) | Matsuya, near Shijo-Omiya, Shimogyo-ku, Kyoto<br>Link: [matsuyafoods.co.jp](https://www.matsuyafoods.co.jp/english/) | ~¥770/adult (approx; part of a ¥2,300 set for three adults that also included miso soup and a small salad) | ~800 kcal (approx, for the full set) | Gyudon is Japan's standard cheap, fast meal: thin-sliced beef and onion simmered in a sweet-savory soy broth and ladled over a bowl of rice, ready in under a minute since the beef is barely thicker than paper. Matsuya, like most gyudon chains, takes orders from a ticket vending machine at the entrance - insert cash or a card, press the button for your bowl, and hand the ticket to staff - rather than ordering at a counter. Eaten hot with chopsticks; contains beef and soy, and is easy to make toddler-friendly by pulling out plain rice and well-cooked beef pieces. |
| Anago-meshi | [25](#day-25---saturday-october-31-2026---miyajima-itsukushima) | Street stalls, Omotesando shopping street, Miyajima-cho, Hatsukaichi (exact stall unverified) | ~¥1,400/adult (approx; bought together with grilled oysters for ¥4,200 total for three adults) | ~600 kcal (approx, for the anago-meshi alone) | Anago-meshi is Miyajima's own local specialty and is not the same fish as the more famous unagi: anago is saltwater conger eel rather than unagi's freshwater eel, milder and lighter in flavor. The eel is grilled and brushed with a sweet soy glaze, then laid over a bowl of rice that has often been steamed with some of the eel's own cooking broth. Eaten hot, off a plate or in a lacquered box; contains fish, soy and wheat (from the glaze), with no raw component - the eel is always cooked through. |
| Fried rice | [10](#day-10---friday-october-16-2026---tokyo) | Hotel kitchenette, Taito-ku (self-catered) | ~¥800/adult (approx; ingredient cost, ¥2,400 for the party's last-night meal) | ~700 kcal | Not a restaurant dish but a simple home-style meal cooked in the room on the final Tokyo night to use up whatever vegetables and eggs were left in the fridge: rice stir-fried in a hot pan with chopped leftover vegetables and beaten egg, seasoned with soy sauce. Eaten hot, straight from the pan; ingredients and seasoning vary by what's left over, but it typically contains egg, soy and whatever vegetables remain, making it easy to adapt for a toddler by leaving out any strong seasoning. |

#### Grilled and skewered

| What | Day | Where | Typical price | kcal | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Yakitori set | [6](#day-6---monday-october-12-2026---tokyo-sports-day) | Torikizoku (flat-price yakitori chain), near Ueno Station, Taito-ku, exact branch unconfirmed<br>Link: [torikizoku.com](https://torikizoku.com/en/) | About ¥1,900 per adult, approximate (the day's dinner came to ¥5,700 for the three adults, covering about five skewers each plus rice and miso soup; Torikizoku prices every skewer at a flat ¥390 including tax) | About 800 kcal for the whole meal, approximate, most of it from the skewers | Yakitori is chicken grilled over charcoal on short bamboo skewers and ordered by the piece rather than as one fixed plate. Different parts of the bird are named and sold separately, thigh, skin, liver, heart, cartilage and tail among them, and each skewer comes brushed with a sweet soy glaze (tare) or simply salted, a choice made when ordering. Torikizoku is a nationwide chain built around this exact format at one flat price per skewer, so a table just keeps ordering a few at a time. Eaten hot, straight off the grill, with rice and miso soup on the side; the plainer cuts (thigh, skin) are easy to share with a toddler once cut small, while liver and heart are adult-only tastes worth trying before serving to a child. |
| Izakaya chicken skewers | [5](#day-5---sunday-october-11-2026---tokyo) | An izakaya (a casual Japanese pub serving small grilled and fried dishes with drinks) in Shibuya, specific shop unconfirmed | About ¥1,270 per adult, approximate (the day's set course of skewers, edamame and rice came to ¥3,800 for three adults) | About 600 kcal for the full course, approximate, with the skewers themselves closer to 350 kcal | Grilled chicken on skewers again, the same charcoal-grilled, salt-or-glaze format as yakitori, served here as part of an early-evening izakaya set alongside edamame (boiled, salted soybeans popped from the pod by hand) and a bowl of rice. Eaten hot and shared around the table. The early, kid-friendly seating time matters more than the food itself: izakayas fill with adult drinkers as the evening goes on, so arriving right when they open keeps it toddler-friendly. |
| Ika-yaki (grilled squid skewer) | [15](#day-15---wednesday-october-21-2026---kyoto) | A food stall at the Kobo-ichi temple market, To-ji, 1 Kujocho, Minami-ku, Kyoto 601-8473 | ¥400-600, typical festival-stall price, approximate | About 150 kcal for one skewer, approximate | Ika-yaki is a whole squid, tentacles included, grilled flat on a griddle or over charcoal on a long stick, brushed with a soy-based sauce as it cooks. It is classic festival and market food, sold from open-air stalls rather than sit-down restaurants, and eaten hot, straight off the stick, standing up. The texture is chewy rather than flaky, closer to calamari than to any fish; it is cooked all the way through, not raw, though the char and chewiness make it a better fit for older toddlers with more chewing experience than for very young ones. |
| Grilled scallop skewer | [8](#day-8---wednesday-october-14-2026---tokyo-odaiba--toyosu) | A stall in the Toyosu Market outer market area, Koto-ku, nearest station Shijo-mae, exact stall unconfirmed | ¥500-800, typical market-stall price, approximate | About 90 kcal per skewer, approximate | A whole scallop, shelled, skewered and grilled over charcoal or a griddle until just cooked through, often brushed with soy sauce or butter. It was eaten here as one item in a grazing breakfast through Toyosu's outer market stalls, alongside rolled omelette and a few pieces of sushi. Served hot immediately off the grill, not raw, and easy to cut into toddler-sized pieces, though shellfish is a common allergen worth checking for first. |
| Charcoal-grilled Hiroshima-bay oysters | [25](#day-25---saturday-october-31-2026---miyajima-itsukushima) | A street stall on Omotesando, Miyajima's main shopping street, Miyajima-cho, Hatsukaichi, exact stall unconfirmed | ¥400-600 for two or three oysters, typical street-stall price, approximate | About 150 kcal for a few oysters, approximate | Grilled oysters are Hiroshima's signature seafood: the bay is Japan's largest oyster-producing area, and Miyajima's Omotesando street is lined with stalls selling them shucked from the shell and grilled over charcoal right in front of customers. They are eaten hot, straight from the grill, often with a squeeze of citrus or a dab of soy sauce, standing at the stall rather than seated. Fully cooked, not raw, but oysters are a shellfish allergen and a common source of stomach upset if undercooked, so buy from a busy, visibly hot grill and let them cool before handing pieces to a toddler. |
| Yakitori | [21](#day-21---tuesday-october-27-2026---kyoto-day-trip-to-uji) | Torikizoku Shijo Omiya, 116 Nishiki Omiyacho, Nakagyo-ku, Kyoto, 4F (postal code unconfirmed)<br>Link: [torikizoku.com](https://torikizoku.com/en/) | About ¥900 per adult, approximate (the day's dinner came to ¥2,700 for three adults, on top of Torikizoku's flat ¥390 per skewer) | About 780 kcal for the meal, approximate, with the skewers themselves closer to 530 kcal | The same flat-price, order-by-the-piece yakitori as Day 6, this time ordered by named cut: momo (thigh), negima (thigh alternated with leek), tsukune (minced chicken formed into a meatball), kawa (crispy grilled skin) and tebasaki (whole chicken wings), each available salted or with the sweet soy glaze. Eaten hot off the grill with a bowl of rice per adult. Tsukune and momo are the easiest cuts to serve to a toddler, cut off the stick and off the bone; tebasaki, being a whole wing, needs the meat pulled off first. |
| Kushikatsu | [29](#day-29---wednesday-november-4-2026---osaka) | Kushikatsu Daruma, 1-6-8 Dotonbori, Chuo-ku, Osaka 542-0071<br>Link: [kushikatu-daruma.com](https://www.kushikatu-daruma.com/) | About ¥1,770 per adult, approximate (the day's set of pork, chicken, lotus root, quail egg and cheese skewers came to ¥5,300 for three adults) | About 750 kcal for the set, approximate | Kushikatsu is bite-sized meat, seafood or vegetables threaded on a skewer, coated in breadcrumbs and deep-fried, Osaka's answer to yakitori and the signature food of the city's Shinsekai district, where this chain began. Each table shares one pot of thick, dark dipping sauce, and it comes with exactly one rule, absolutely enforced and posted on signs throughout the restaurant: dip once, before you take a bite, and never dip a skewer a second time after it has touched your mouth. It is the single most-broken piece of etiquette in Osaka's restaurants, and staff will say something if they see it happen. Shredded raw cabbage sits on the table for free refills and doubles as an edible scoop for extra sauce without redipping. Eaten hot, one skewer at a time; the sauce itself is savory and slightly sweet, not spicy, so a toddler can eat the fried meat and vegetables with the sauce wiped off. |

#### Seafood and sushi

| What | Day | Where | Typical price | kcal | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Sushi and seafood grazing | [8](#day-8---wednesday-october-14-2026---tokyo-odaiba--toyosu) | Stalls in the Toyosu Market outer market area, Koto-ku, nearest station Shijo-mae, exact stalls unconfirmed | About ¥1,200 per adult, approximate (the day's grazing breakfast came to ¥3,600 for three adults) | About 550 kcal for the full grazing breakfast, approximate | A walk-and-eat breakfast through the food stalls just outside Tokyo's Toyosu wholesale fish market, picking up nigiri sushi (a hand-pressed finger of vinegared rice topped with a slice of fish, held together with no wrapping), a grilled scallop skewer, and tamagoyaki, a sweetened rolled omelette, cut into slices. This is not the conveyor-belt kind of sushi; each piece is pressed to order at a stall counter and eaten standing there. The nigiri here is raw fish on rice, so anyone deciding what a toddler can eat should treat it as an adult item, while the grilled scallop and the cooked omelette are both safe, kid-friendly picks from the same stalls. |
| Shirasu-don | [7](#day-7---tuesday-october-13-2026---kamakura-day-trip) | A restaurant on Komachi-dori, Kamakura, specific shop unconfirmed | About ¥1,200 per adult, approximate (the day's lunch came to ¥3,600 for three adults) | About 700 kcal for the bowl, with rice and miso soup | Shirasu-don is a Kamakura coastal specialty: a bowl of rice piled high with shirasu, tiny whitebait only a centimetre or two long, served either raw and glistening or briefly boiled until opaque, often with both styles mixed on the same bowl split down the middle. Eaten with a splash of soy sauce and a squeeze of ginger, and served with miso soup on the side. The raw version is, straightforwardly, raw fish, small enough that it can look harmless but should be treated with the same caution as any raw seafood for a toddler; the boiled shirasu on the same bowl is a safer alternative to point a child toward. |
| Kakinoha-zushi | [20](#day-20---monday-october-26-2026---kyoto-day-trip-to-nara) | A restaurant near Nara Park, Nara, specific shop unconfirmed | ¥800-1,200 for a set of a few pieces, typical menu price, approximate | About 450 kcal for a typical set, approximate (the day's lunch of kakinoha-zushi and a kudzu-mochi dessert together came to 650 kcal) | Kakinoha-zushi is Nara's regional sushi: a slice of cured mackerel or salmon pressed onto a small block of vinegared rice and wrapped tightly in a persimmon leaf, which is folded around it and left to sit for a few hours before serving. The leaf is a preservation technique from before refrigeration, its natural antibacterial properties and the pressure of the wrapping helped the fish keep in Nara's inland heat, and it is not eaten; unwrap it and discard it before eating the sushi inside. The fish is cured with salt and vinegar rather than cooked, so it sits in the same raw-fish category as sashimi for anyone deciding whether it suits a toddler, even though it no longer looks or tastes raw. |
| Nigiri sushi and fresh oysters | [29](#day-29---wednesday-november-4-2026---osaka) | Kuromon Ichiba Market, 2-4-1 Nipponbashi, Chuo-ku, Osaka 542-0073<br>Link: [kuromon.com](https://kuromon.com/en/) | Nigiri sushi typically ¥100-500 per piece; oysters typically ¥300-600 each, shucked to order, approximate | About 400 kcal for a few pieces of each, approximate (the day's full grazing lunch, which also included grilled wagyu skewers and tamagoyaki, came to 700 kcal) | Kuromon Ichiba is a roughly 580-metre covered market in central Osaka, originally a wholesale fish and produce market that today sells single portions meant to be eaten standing at the counter. Nigiri sushi here is the same hand-pressed rice-and-raw-fish format as elsewhere, a slice of fish laid over a finger of rice with no seaweed wrapper, while the oysters are served on the half shell, freshly shucked and eaten raw with a squeeze of lemon, not cooked. Both the sushi and the oysters are raw seafood and a shellfish allergen risk on top of that for the oysters, so they are an adult treat here rather than something to share bite-for-bite with a toddler. |

#### Hot pot

| What | Day | Where | Typical price | kcal | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Yosenabe | [12](#day-12---sunday-october-18-2026---kawaguchiko) | K's House Mt. Fuji shared guest kitchen, Fujikawaguchiko | About ¥1,000 per adult, ingredient cost for a self-catered meal (the day's dinner ingredients came to ¥3,000 for three adults) | About 700 kcal for the meal, approximate | Yosenabe is a mixed hot pot: a pot of broth kept gently simmering on a portable burner at the table, into which thin-sliced pork, napa cabbage, shiitake and enoki mushrooms, tofu and spring onion go in batches, cooked a few minutes at a time and eaten straight out of the pot as they finish rather than served all at once. It is a late-autumn dish in Japan, timed to the first cold weeks of the season, and this version was cooked in the hostel's shared guest kitchen using ingredients bought a few days earlier. Once the meat and vegetables are eaten, udon noodles go into the leftover broth to finish it as a last course rather than let the broth go to waste. The main hazard is the pot itself: it sits at a rolling simmer for the whole meal, which is scalding at toddler standing or reaching height, so it needs to stay out of a small child's reach with only an adult ladling out portions, and those portions should be cooled before a toddler eats them. |

#### Street snacks

| What | Day | Where | Typical price | kcal | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Takoyaki | [3](#day-3---friday-october-9-2026---tokyo) | Ameyoko market, Taito-ku<br>Link: [ameyoko.net](https://www.ameyoko.net/) | ~¥600/serving of 6-8 balls (approximate; the day's ¥2,150 party cost covered takoyaki, gyoza and yakitori together, not takoyaki alone) | ~250 kcal (approximate; the day's grazing lunch of takoyaki, gyoza and yakitori together totalled 700 kcal/adult) | Takoyaki are balls of wheat batter cooked in a dimpled iron pan, each with a small piece of octopus inside, flipped with a pick until round and crisp outside. Served hot in a paper tray, topped with a sweet-savoury brown sauce, mayonnaise squeezed in a zigzag, a dusting of dried green seaweed (aonori) and shavings of dried bonito (katsuobushi) that visibly curl and wave in the rising heat, which surprises first-time diners into thinking the topping is alive. The centre stays molten well after the outside has cooled, and it will burn a mouth that rushes it - worth knowing generally, and especially with a toddler, who should get a piece bitten in half and left to cool rather than a whole ball. Contains shellfish/octopus. |
| Menchi-katsu, senbei, croquette | [6](#day-6---monday-october-12-2026---tokyo-sports-day) | Yanaka Ginza, Taito-ku<br>Link: [yanakaginza.com](https://www.yanakaginza.com/) | Menchi-katsu ~¥250-350 each; senbei ~¥150-250 each; croquette ~¥150-200 each (approximate; the day's combined lunch cost ¥1,850 for three adults sharing all three) | ~650 kcal combined per adult across all three snacks (approximate, taken from the day's meal total, not split out by dish) | Three separate stall snacks eaten walking down this old-fashioned shopping street. Menchi-katsu is a flat, hand-sized patty of minced meat (usually a beef-pork mix) coated in breadcrumbs and deep-fried, served plain in a paper sleeve to eat straight from the hand while walking - hot, greasy, and one of the most common Japanese hand-food snacks. Senbei are savoury rice crackers, usually brushed with a soy-sauce glaze and grilled until firm and lightly charred at the edges; despite looking like a sweet biscuit, they are not sweet at all, which catches people out the first time. Croquette (korokke) is a breaded, deep-fried patty of mashed potato mixed with minced meat or vegetables, softer inside than menchi-katsu. All three are hot, eaten by hand, and hard rather than a texture to hand a very young toddler unsupervised - better broken into small pieces first. |
| Nikuman (steamed pork buns) | [9](#day-9---thursday-october-15-2026---yokohama-day-trip) | Yokohama Chinatown, Naka-ku | ~¥300-400 per bun (approximate, typical Chinatown counter price; the day's full grazing lunch of buns and skewers together cost ¥4,300 for three adults) | ~150-200 kcal per bun (approximate; the day's full grazing lunch of two nikuman plus a char siu skewer and an ebi-mayo bun together totalled 800 kcal/adult) | Nikuman are fist-sized steamed buns made from soft, slightly sweet wheat dough, filled with seasoned minced pork and its own juices, sold hot from steaming stacks in dozens of stall windows along Chinatown's main strip. They are eaten by hand, straight from a paper wrapper, no chopsticks needed - Yokohama Chinatown is where this trip eats them, since a fresh one sold hot off the steamer beats almost any pre-packaged version. Winter is nikuman season at conbini and street counters generally, but Chinatown sells them year-round. Easy for a toddler once torn into smaller pieces and cooled, though the filling can be hotter than the bun suggests. |
| Yakisoba, ika-yaki, taiyaki | [15](#day-15---wednesday-october-21-2026---kyoto) | Kobo-ichi market, To-ji<br>Link: [toji.or.jp](https://toji.or.jp/) | Yakisoba ~¥500-600; ika-yaki skewer ~¥400-500; taiyaki ~¥200-300 (approximate; the day's combined lunch of all three cost ¥5,400 for three adults) | ~750 kcal combined per adult across all three (approximate, from the day's meal total, not split by dish) | Three stall foods eaten at this monthly temple market. Yakisoba is stir-fried wheat noodles with shredded pork and cabbage, tossed in a thick, sweet-savoury brown sauce and served in a paper tray, eaten with a small fork or pick - Japan's everyday fairground noodle dish. Ika-yaki is a whole squid, split flat, skewered and grilled over charcoal, brushed with a soy-based glaze - chewier than most toddler food and best cut into small pieces rather than handed over on the skewer. Taiyaki is a fish-shaped cake, its batter cooked in a hinged iron mould so the outside comes out crisp and golden like a waffle, filled with sweet red bean paste (sometimes custard) that stays hot in the centre for a minute or two after it's handed over. |
| Takoyaki, warabimochi, hojicha | [19](#day-19---sunday-october-25-2026---kyoto) | Tenjin-ichi market, Kitano Tenmangu<br>Link: [kitanotenmangu.or.jp](https://kitanotenmangu.or.jp/) | Takoyaki ~¥500-600; warabimochi cup ~¥400-500; hojicha cup ~¥200-300 (approximate; the day's combined lunch of all three cost ¥3,600 for three adults) | ~700 kcal combined per adult across all three (approximate, from the day's meal total) | Takoyaki here is the same griddled octopus-batter ball described above (see Day 3) - same molten-centre burn risk. Warabimochi is a bracken-starch jelly, cut into soft, jiggly cubes and dusted heavily with kinako (roasted soybean flour), eaten cold with a toothpick or small fork; it is much softer and less chewy than the pounded-rice mochi more familiar to Western readers, so it is a reasonably safe toddler texture once cut small. Hojicha is green tea that has been roasted rather than left green-leaf, giving it a reddish-brown colour and a toasty, less bitter flavour than sencha or matcha - served hot in a paper cup, a gentler introduction to Japanese tea for anyone who finds matcha too bitter. |
| Takoyaki, plus negiyaki or kushikatsu | [27](#day-27---monday-november-2-2026---hiroshima-to-osaka) | Dotonbori, Chuo-ku, Osaka | Takoyaki ~¥500-700/serving; negiyaki ~¥600-800; kushikatsu ~¥150-250/skewer (approximate; the day's combined dinner cost ¥2,400 for three adults) | ~750 kcal combined per adult (approximate, from the day's meal total) | Takoyaki is the same octopus-batter ball described above. Negiyaki is a close cousin of okonomiyaki, made the same way - batter and egg cooked on a griddle - but built around a thick layer of chopped green onion (negi) instead of cabbage, and usually eaten with a lighter soy-based sauce rather than the thick brown okonomiyaki sauce. Kushikatsu is bite-sized meat, seafood or vegetables, breaded and deep-fried on a skewer; in Osaka it is dipped once into a shared sauce pot at the counter, with a strict no-double-dipping rule enforced by staff - a bitten skewer never goes back in the pot. |
| Takoyaki and okonomiyaki | [28](#day-28---tuesday-november-3-2026---osaka) | Dotonbori, Chuo-ku, Osaka | Takoyaki ~¥500-600/serving; okonomiyaki ~¥800-1,200 to share (approximate; the day's combined dinner cost ¥3,200 for three adults) | ~750 kcal combined per adult (approximate, from the day's meal total) | Takoyaki, again, is the griddled octopus-batter ball - the molten centre stays scalding for a minute or two after serving, so let it cool before handing one to a toddler. Okonomiyaki is a savoury pancake: in this Osaka version, batter, shredded cabbage, egg and a chosen filling (usually pork belly) are all mixed together before cooking, unlike the layered Hiroshima-style version this trip eats earlier (Days 25-26) - same name, genuinely different dish. It is cooked on a flat griddle, cut into wedges at the table, and finished with a thick brown sauce, mayonnaise, aonori seaweed flakes and dancing bonito shavings, the same toppings as takoyaki. |

#### Konbini, bento and set meals

The everyday backbone of eating in Japan, and what most of this trip's lunches are. This
is the one section that is not restaurants: onigiri, bento, eki-ben and karaage come from
convenience-store chains (7-Eleven, FamilyMart, Lawson), station kiosks and supermarket
delis rather than from a single named shop, so the website given below is the chain's own
site, and Where names the district rather than an address.

| What | Day | Where | Typical price | kcal | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Onigiri, rice balls with a filling wrapped in nori | [1](#day-1---wednesday-october-7-2026---tokyo-narita-arrival), [2](#day-2---thursday-october-8-2026---tokyo), [5](#day-5---sunday-october-11-2026---tokyo), [16](#day-16---thursday-october-22-2026---kyoto), [27](#day-27---monday-november-2-2026---hiroshima-to-osaka) | Conbini and station counters throughout<br>Link: 7-Eleven: [sej.co.jp](https://www.sej.co.jp/); FamilyMart: [family.co.jp](https://www.family.co.jp/english.html); Lawson: [lawson.co.jp](https://www.lawson.co.jp/) | ¥130-200 each | ~150-200 kcal each (approximate) | Onigiri is a hand-sized ball of plain white rice, usually pressed into a rounded triangle, with a savoury filling (salmon, tuna mayonnaise, pickled plum, cod roe) tucked into the middle. A strip of crisp seaweed (nori) wraps the outside; a clever plastic sleeve keeps the nori separated from the moist rice until the wrapper is pulled open in a specific order, so the seaweed is still crisp rather than soggy at the first bite - worth showing a first-time eater once. Eaten cold, straight from the hand, no utensils needed - the cheapest, most portable food in Japan and sold everywhere from conbini to train kiosks. A good toddler food once the filling is checked (some are spicy or bony). |
| Konbini bento | [2](#day-2---thursday-october-8-2026---tokyo), [5](#day-5---sunday-october-11-2026---tokyo), [11](#day-11---saturday-october-17-2026---tokyo--kawaguchiko), [16](#day-16---thursday-october-22-2026---kyoto), [19](#day-19---sunday-october-25-2026---kyoto), [22](#day-22---wednesday-october-28-2026---kyoto-day-trip-to-the-fushimi-sake-district) | FamilyMart, Lawson and 7-Eleven throughout<br>Link: 7-Eleven: [sej.co.jp](https://www.sej.co.jp/); FamilyMart: [family.co.jp](https://www.family.co.jp/english.html); Lawson: [lawson.co.jp](https://www.lawson.co.jp/) | ¥580-750, heated at the counter on request; the same boxes go to half price late in the evening | ~600-750 kcal (approximate, typical for a rice-plus-main box) | A partitioned plastic box of rice, a main (fried chicken, grilled fish, pork cutlet) and two or three small side dishes (pickles, a vegetable simmer, tamagoyaki), bought refrigerated off a shelf and microwaved by the cashier on request, in a few seconds, right at the counter. Eaten with disposable chopsticks, hot or cold depending on choice. The same boxes are marked down by up to 50% ("hangaku") in the evening as the store approaches closing stock rotation, which this trip uses more than once for a cheap late supply run. |
| Eki-ben, the station and train bento | [11](#day-11---saturday-october-17-2026---tokyo--kawaguchiko), [14](#day-14---tuesday-october-20-2026---kawaguchiko--kyoto), [24](#day-24---friday-october-30-2026---kyoto-to-hiroshima), [27](#day-27---monday-november-2-2026---hiroshima-to-osaka) | Station concourses before the long legs | ¥900-1,500 | ~700 kcal (approximate, from the day files' meal figures for eki-ben lunches) | Eki-ben ("station bento") is a boxed meal sold specifically at train stations and designed to be eaten cold, at your seat, while the train is moving - no reheating expected or needed. Each region sells its own eki-ben built around local ingredients (a particular fish, a particular vegetable, a particular rice), so the box itself is part of the souvenir of wherever it was bought, and the same eki-ben is rarely sold two stations away. Bought at kiosks and dedicated eki-ben counters rather than a chain with one website, which is why no venue link is given here. |
| Karaage, marinated fried chicken | [3](#day-3---friday-october-9-2026---tokyo), [16](#day-16---thursday-october-22-2026---kyoto) | Conbini hot counters and supermarket delis<br>Link: 7-Eleven: [sej.co.jp](https://www.sej.co.jp/); FamilyMart: [family.co.jp](https://www.family.co.jp/english.html); Lawson: [lawson.co.jp](https://www.lawson.co.jp/) | Sold by the piece at conbini counters (~¥120-200/piece), by weight at supermarket delis, where it is a common half-price item | ~200-250 kcal per serving (approximate) | Karaage is bite-sized chicken, marinated in soy sauce, ginger and garlic, coated lightly in starch and deep-fried until the outside is craggy and dark gold. Sold hot and ready-made from a heated case by the conbini register (grabbed with tongs and a paper bag) or by weight from a supermarket's prepared-food counter, eaten by hand as a snack or added to a bento. Different from the pale, panko-crumbed cutlet (tonkatsu or katsu) also on this trip - karaage has no breadcrumb coating, just a thin starch dust. |
| Teishoku, a set meal of a main with rice, miso soup and pickles | [4](#day-4---saturday-october-10-2026---tokyo), [17](#day-17---friday-october-23-2026---kyoto), [21](#day-21---tuesday-october-27-2026---kyoto-day-trip-to-uji), [22](#day-22---wednesday-october-28-2026---kyoto-day-trip-to-the-fushimi-sake-district), [23](#day-23---thursday-october-29-2026---kyoto-low-key-day--packing), [24](#day-24---friday-october-30-2026---kyoto-to-hiroshima), [26](#day-26---sunday-november-1-2026---hiroshima-city-day) | Yayoiken-style chains and neighbourhood diners<br>Link: [yayoiken.com](https://www.yayoiken.com/en/) | ¥800-1,200 | ~750-800 kcal (approximate, typical for these meals in the day files) | Teishoku is the standard Japanese lunch format: one tray holding a main dish (grilled fish, a stir-fry, a cutlet), a bowl of plain white rice, a small bowl of miso soup, and a few pickled vegetables (tsukemono), all served together rather than as separate courses. Rice and soup are often free to refill at chains like Yayoiken. It is the most reliable way to eat a nutritionally balanced meal cheaply in Japan, and this trip eats it often - the mix of main dish changes, but the tray format (rice, soup, pickles, one main) stays the same every time. |
| Grilled mackerel or salmon | 16, and most self-catered breakfasts | Supermarket fish counters, and teishoku sets | A fillet is ¥200-350 at a supermarket | ~200-300 kcal per fillet (approximate) | A single fish fillet, salted and grilled plain (no sauce, no breading) until the skin is lightly charred, served alongside rice and miso soup either as a self-cooked breakfast or as the main in a teishoku set. It is the plainest, most everyday hot dish on this whole trip - the same fillet a Japanese household might grill on any ordinary morning - and an easy, low-risk food for a toddler once any small bones are checked for. |

#### Sweets and tea

| What | Day | Where | Typical price | kcal | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mitarashi dango | [2](#day-2---thursday-october-8-2026---tokyo) | Nakamise-dori, Asakusa | ~¥400-500 per skewer (day file: ¥1,200 for three adults, one skewer each) | ~100 kcal per skewer | Dango are small balls of pounded rice flour, chewy and dense rather than soft, threaded three to five onto a short bamboo skewer. Mitarashi dango are grilled just enough to lightly char the outside, then brushed with a glossy, sweet-and-salty soy glaze. Eaten cold or just-warm, straight off the skewer. The chewy, slightly sticky texture is worth flagging for a toddler - it is closer to a firm gummy sweet than to bread or cake, so bite-sized pieces pulled off the skewer are safer than the whole ball. |
| Harajuku crepe | [5](#day-5---sunday-october-11-2026---tokyo) | Marion Crêpes, Takeshita-dori, Jingumae, Shibuya-ku<br>Link: [marion.co.jp](https://www.marion.co.jp/) | ~¥500/adult (day file: ¥1,500 for three adults) | ~350 kcal | A thin, sweet crepe made on a hot griddle, folded around whipped cream and fruit (here, strawberry) into a cone shape and wrapped in paper to eat one-handed while walking. Marion Crêpes opened the first storefront on Takeshita Street in 1976 and is credited with starting the walking-crepe format as Harajuku's signature street food; the chain now has around 110 shops nationwide, but this is the original location. Cold cream inside a warm wrapper, eaten by hand, no utensils. |
| Matcha and wagashi | [4](#day-4---saturday-october-10-2026---tokyo) | Hamarikyu Gardens teahouse, Chuo-ku<br>Link: [tokyo-park.or.jp](https://www.tokyo-park.or.jp/park/hama-rikyu/index.html) | ~¥1,000 for a bowl of matcha with a wagashi sweet (typical rate at the garden's Nakajima teahouse) | ~150 kcal (approximate) | Served on a wooden platform teahouse reached by a bridge over the garden's tidal pond. Matcha is green tea leaves ground to a fine powder and whisked directly into hot water with a bamboo whisk, rather than steeped as loose leaves - it is thick, a little frothy, and noticeably more bitter than the green tea most Western readers know. It is traditionally paired with a wagashi, a small, usually bean-paste-based sweet, eaten first specifically to balance the tea's bitterness - wagashi themselves are far less sweet than a Western dessert, built around the mild, earthy flavour of sweetened red bean rather than sugar or chocolate. |
| Matcha and a seasonal sweet | [17](#day-17---friday-october-23-2026---kyoto) | Okochi Sanso Garden, Ukyo-ku<br>Link: [okochisansogarden.com](https://okochisansogarden.com/en) | Included in the ¥1,000/adult garden admission | ~150 kcal (approximate) | A bowl of whisked matcha and a small seasonal wagashi sweet, served on a viewing terrace at the end of the walk through this hillside villa garden, included in the admission price rather than charged separately. Same bitter-tea, mild-sweet pairing as above - the terrace view over Arashiyama's hills is much of the point of stopping here rather than skipping straight past. |
| Matcha soft-serve | [18](#day-18---saturday-october-24-2026---kyoto) | Higashiyama-ku, Kyoto | ~¥500 (approximate; day's combined lunch of zaru soba plus matcha soft-serve cost ¥2,300 for three adults) | ~200 kcal (approximate) | Soft-serve ice cream flavoured and coloured with matcha powder, in a cone or cup - a modern, cold, kid-friendly way to try matcha's flavour without the bitterness or ceremony of a whisked bowl. Bought from a shop window in the lanes leading up to Kiyomizu-dera, eaten walking, no raw fish or allergens beyond dairy to flag. |
| Taihoan tea ceremony | [21](#day-21---tuesday-october-27-2026---kyoto-day-trip-to-uji) | Uji-shi, Kyoto<br>Link: [kyoto-uji-kankou.or.jp](https://www.kyoto-uji-kankou.or.jp/taihoan-en.html) | ¥1,000/adult, whisk your own matcha, low tatami seating with no chair option | ~50-80 kcal for the bowl of tea and its accompanying sweet (approximate) | Taihoan is Uji's municipal tea house, built for visitors to try the formal version of matcha properly rather than just drink it: a host guides each visitor through whisking their own bowl, paired with a seasonal wagashi. Uji has been Japan's most famous green-tea-growing region since medieval times, so this is the trip's one hands-on version of the matcha already drunk elsewhere as a garden extra. Seating is on low tatami mats with no chair option, which is worth knowing before booking if kneeling or sitting on the floor is difficult for anyone in the party; toddlers are welcome alongside a seated parent. |
| Kudzu-mochi | [20](#day-20---monday-october-26-2026---kyoto-day-trip-to-nara) | near Nara Park | ~¥600-700 (approximate; day's combined lunch of kakinoha-zushi plus kudzu-mochi cost ¥2,700 for three adults) | ~100 kcal (approximate) | Kudzu-mochi is a Nara speciality despite the name "mochi": rather than pounded rice, it is a chilled, wobbly jelly set from kudzu (arrowroot) starch, cut into cubes and dusted with kinako (roasted soybean flour), usually with a little dark sugar syrup poured over. It is cold, very soft and slippery rather than chewy - easier for a toddler to manage than rice-based mochi, though the cubes are still a choking-hazard size and shape unless cut smaller. |
| Momiji manju | [25](#day-25---saturday-october-31-2026---miyajima-itsukushima) | Miyajima | ~¥150-200 each (approximate, typical Miyajima shop price) | ~100 kcal each (approximate) | A small, maple-leaf-shaped sponge cake, cooked in a hinged mould the same way as taiyaki, usually filled with sweet red bean paste (sometimes custard or chocolate). Miyajima is famous for its maple trees as well as its floating torii gate, and this cake is the island's signature souvenir sweet, sold in boxes at nearly every shop along Omotesando as well as individually for eating on the spot. Soft cake exterior, no shell or wrapper to remove, an easy toddler-sized sweet once cooled. |

#### Regional specialities

| What | Day | Where | Typical price | kcal | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Houtou | [12](#day-12---sunday-october-18-2026---kawaguchiko) | Hoto Fudou (chain, exact branch unconfirmed), Fujikawaguchiko area, Yamanashi<br>Link: [houtou-fudou.jp](http://www.houtou-fudou.jp/) | ¥1,400/adult, approximate (the day's lunch cost ¥4,200 for three adults) | ~750 kcal, approximate | Yamanashi's own noodle dish, thick, flat, hand-cut wheat noodles, wider than udon, simmered directly in a hearty miso broth with kabocha squash and root vegetables, so the noodles thicken the broth rather than being served separately. It comes to the table in one large iron pot, still simmering, and is eaten hot straight from it with everyone ladling their own bowlful. Nothing raw, no fish, and the soft-cooked squash and noodles are easy to cut small for a toddler once cooled. |
| Yudofu | [17](#day-17---friday-october-23-2026---kyoto) | Restaurant unverified, Saga-Arashiyama, Ukyo-ku, near Tenryu-ji | ¥1,000/adult, approximate (the day's lunch cost ¥3,100 for three adults) | ~700 kcal, approximate | Simple blocks of soft tofu simmered gently in a pot of plain kombu (kelp) broth at the table, kept warm over a small burner, and lifted out piece by piece with a ladle or chopsticks to dip in a soy-based sauce with grated ginger and spring onion. Originally a Buddhist temple dish, it is deliberately plain rather than richly flavoured, and it is the speciality of this exact Arashiyama temple district. Vegetarian at its base, though the dipping sauce may contain fish stock, and the soft, plain tofu is one of the easiest dishes in Japan to share with a toddler once cooled. |
| Obanzai | [18](#day-18---saturday-october-24-2026---kyoto) | Obanzai restaurant, Gion, Higashiyama-ku (restaurant name unverified) | ¥1,200/adult, approximate (the day's set dinner cost ¥3,600 for three adults) | ~800 kcal, approximate | Kyoto's home-style cooking: a set of several small vegetable-forward side dishes served together on individual small plates rather than one main course, typically simmered vegetables, a little tofu, pickles, and a small piece of grilled or simmered fish, all seasonal and lightly seasoned. It is what Kyoto households actually eat day to day, closer to a home-cooked spread than restaurant food built for visitors. Nothing raw and no strong spice, and the small varied portions make it easy to pick out soft, plain pieces for a toddler. |
| Kaiseki dinner | [13](#day-13---monday-october-19-2026---kawaguchiko) | Lakeland Hotel Mizunosato, 1070 Kodachi, Fujikawaguchiko, Yamanashi<br>Link: [mizunosato.jp](https://www.mizunosato.jp/en/) | ¥1,980/adult, the ryokan's half-board dinner add-on rate (a full a la carte kaiseki elsewhere typically costs several times this) | ~750 kcal, approximate, across all courses | The formal multi-course Japanese dinner, served here as the ryokan's evening meal: a small seasonal appetiser first, then sashimi (thin-sliced raw fish served cold with soy sauce and wasabi), a grilled dish, a simmered vegetable dish, tempura (battered, deep-fried vegetables or seafood), then rice with pickles and miso soup, finishing with a piece of seasonal fruit. Courses arrive one at a time over an hour or more rather than all at once, following set seasonal presentation rather than an a la carte menu. It contains raw fish in the sashimi course, and the length of the meal is worth planning around infant bedtime; the cooked rice, simmered vegetables and miso soup are fine to share with a toddler once cooled, the sashimi is not. |
| Kasujiru | [22](#day-22---wednesday-october-28-2026---kyoto-day-trip-to-the-fushimi-sake-district) | Restaurant near the Gekkeikan Okura Sake Museum, Chushojima, Fushimi-ku (address unverified) | ¥1,100/adult, approximate for the full set meal (the day's lunch cost ¥3,300 for three adults, including rice, pickles and fish; the soup itself is a small part of that) | ~350 kcal, approximate, for the soup alone | A thick, savoury soup made from sake kasu, the soft white paste of fermented rice and yeast left behind once sake is pressed out, simmered with pork and root vegetables such as daikon and carrot in a base similar to miso soup. It has a distinct, faintly boozy aroma from the sake lees, though most of the alcohol cooks off in simmering, and it is served hot in a bowl as part of a set meal. It is Fushimi's own dish, tied to this district's sake breweries and not a standard menu item elsewhere in Japan; given the residual alcohol, it is not recommended for infants or toddlers. |
| Anago-meshi | [25](#day-25---saturday-october-31-2026---miyajima-itsukushima) | Omotesando shopping street, Miyajima-cho, Hatsukaichi (street stalls, address unverified) | ¥2,000-3,000/adult, normal menu price, approximate (the day's ¥4,200 lunch for three adults also included grilled oysters) | ~650 kcal, approximate | Grilled saltwater conger eel (anago) laid over a bowl of steamed rice and brushed with a sweet soy glaze, Miyajima's own speciality and a different fish from unagi, the freshwater eel more familiar abroad - anago is lighter and milder in both flavour and texture. It is served hot and fully cooked, not raw, usually in a lacquered box or bowl. The flaky, boneless fish is easy to break into small pieces for a toddler once cooled. |
| Hiroshima-style okonomiyaki | [25](#day-25---saturday-october-31-2026---miyajima-itsukushima) | Okonomimura, 5-13 Shintenchi, Naka-ku, Hiroshima 730-0034<br>Link: [okonomimura.jp](http://www.okonomimura.jp/) | ¥900/adult, approximate (the day's dinner cost ¥2,700 for three adults) | ~750 kcal, approximate | A savoury griddle dish built up in distinct layers rather than mixed together: a thin, crepe-like batter base goes down first, then a tall mound of shredded cabbage, then pork, then a nest of fried yakisoba noodles pressed in as its own layer, then a fried egg on top, the whole stack finished with a thick, slightly sweet brown sauce. Okonomimura is a purpose-built building of stacked okonomiyaki counters, three floors of small individual stalls each with its own griddle, so the dish is cooked to order right in front of diners rather than in a back kitchen. It is eaten hot straight off the griddle with a small metal spatula, contains egg and wheat, and the layered cabbage and noodles cook down soft enough to cut into small toddler bites. |
